Both need the change permission. No limit (project owner, 2026-10-13): only an administrator with the change permission can send it, and only to the own address.
* Versions: every save or restore that changes the subject or text writes an `EmailTextVersion` (subject, text, time, internal account for the audit, cleared when the account is deleted). The page "Versions" (view permission) lists them newest first with the text and marks the current one; "Restore" (change permission, POST only) puts an earlier text or the default back after validating it with the current rules (a version that is no longer valid is refused) and writes a new version and a log entry. The account is not shown; the full history with accounts stays for system administrators (D-018). The latest 100 versions per email are kept.
* Security review of 2026-10-13 (agent `security-reviewer`, read only, started by the main session; no critical or high finding) and how it was handled: M-1 (fixed: stricter deny-list, also for the subject, see above; a fixed non-editable footer was considered and not done); M-2 (fixed: a restore is always logged, also if a preview field is posted); M-3 (accepted by the decision of the project owner that the password change notification gets no special treatment: an administrator can rewrite its warning sentence; the default text contains it); M-4 (open: a refused request still creates one counter row and one suppressed job, so the growth follows the request rate; the reverse proxy must limit the request rate, `docs/INSTALLATION.md`); M-5 (fixed: separate instance-wide budgets for registration and password reset); L-1 (fixed: one check of character categories for subject and text); L-2 (fixed: 100 versions per email); L-3 (fixed: the row is locked when saving); L-4 (fixed: notification jobs are kept 24 hours, D-038); L-5 (open, accepted as low: if sending the information email to an existing address fails, the retry finds its daily budget used and the email is lost); L-6 (accepted by the project owner: no limit for test emails; only an administrator with the change permission can send them, and only to the own address); L-7 (fixed: the editor default has no contact branch if the instance has no website); L-8 (accepted: the list creates the four empty rows on first view, idempotent). Tests for the findings were added and the whole suite passes (519 tests); the corrected version was not reviewed again.
* To be confirmed by the project owner: nothing open for this decision; the permission is not part of any default role (see risks).
* Sources: Django documentation of the model admin (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/ref/contrib/admin/); OWASP Server Side Template Injection Prevention Cheat Sheet (https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Server_Side_Template_Injection_Prevention_Cheat_Sheet.html, checked 2026-10-13): untrusted text must never become template source, syntax must not be filtered out with a denylist, and editing of user-supplied templates is limited to authorised roles and logged for audit. This feature follows it by not using a template engine for the written text at all (fixed placeholders are replaced by plain string substitution; the validation is an allowlist of the placeholder names, not a filter for template syntax), by the permission and by the log entries; D-012, D-018, D-038.

## D-040: Password Change and Two-Factor Authentication with an Authenticator App

* Date: 2026-10-13
* Status: Proposed (implemented and reviewed twice by the `security-reviewer`; the values under "To be confirmed by the project owner" were decided on 2026-10-13, see below). Real email delivery could not be tested; everything is tested with Django's in-memory email backend only.
* Context: README "Current Status", items 1 and 2: password change, the TOTP flow, central two-factor enforcement, recovery codes, re-authentication and OTP-protected admin access (D-004, D-016, D-017, D-018).
* Decision:
  * Password change (`/profile/password/`): asks for the current password and the new one twice (same policy as everywhere). The current password is checked through Django's `authenticate()`, so the check runs under the login abuse protection (Axes account counter and separate IP counter, D-017): a hijacked session cannot guess the password through this form. After saving, Django's `update_session_auth_hash()` renews the session ID and keeps this session; every other session ends because Django derives the session hash from the password. The account receives the existing notification (D-038). The administration's own password change page redirects to this one.
  * Second factor: own device models that follow the interface of `django-otp` (`Device`; its `OTPMiddleware` and device functions work with them): `AuthenticatorDevice` (TOTP: 30 seconds, six digits, SHA-1, which is what authenticator apps support; one step of tolerance; a used code cannot be used again) and `RecoveryCodeDevice` with `RecoveryCode`. The security values are not stored in plain text and cannot be shown again (project owner, 2026-10-13: "analogous to the Graph secret, no security value directly visible"): the TOTP key (160 bits) is encrypted with Fernet under a key derived from `SECRET_KEY` with HKDF and its own label (`leg_platform/accounts/encryption.py`, the same scheme as D-006; keys from `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS` are tried for decryption and a key found under an old key is encrypted again with the current one when it is used; a key that cannot be decrypted verifies nothing and is logged); a recovery code is stored only as an HMAC-SHA256 digest with a purpose-specific key (D-023), which is stronger than encryption for a value that is only ever compared. Replacing `SECRET_KEY` without keeping the old one in `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS` makes the second factors unusable, so that those accounts need a reset. The device throttling of django-otp is not used (see "Security review", M-1); the number of failures is limited by the login abuse protection. The issuer shown in the app is `INSTANCE_NAME`. An account has at most one authenticator app; only a confirmed authenticator app counts as a second factor (recovery codes alone do not).
  * Central check (`leg_platform/accounts/second_factor.py`): `requires_second_factor()` (staff users and system administrators), `has_second_factor()`, `is_second_factor_complete()` (django-otp's `is_verified()`; fails closed if the middleware is missing). `SecondFactorMiddleware` (after `OTPMiddleware` and the messages middleware) gives a session of an account that must use a second factor no privileges without it: only logging out and the setup pages are open; an account with a second factor whose session did not complete it is logged out; an account without one is sent to the setup page. A promotion takes effect in an open session. The administration additionally checks the completed second factor in `PortalAdminSite.has_permission()`, so it stays closed if the middleware is removed by mistake. The administration has no login page of its own: it leads to the portal login. TOTP-specific parts are in `totp.py`.
  * Login in two steps (D-017): the password is checked by the login form; if the account has a second factor, no `login()` is called. The session ID is renewed and a pending login (account, time, safe redirect target) is stored in the session; the IP slot of the password attempt is released. `/login/two-factor/` asks for the code (or a recovery code). Each code attempt goes through the Axes lockout check (`AxesStandaloneBackend`), reserves its own IP slot, and a wrong code is a failed attempt of the account and the IP address (Django's `user_login_failed` signal); Django's `login()` is called with the device only after the code was verified, which renews the session ID again and lets django-otp store the device in the session. The pending login lasts 5 minutes (proposal), ends if the account is no longer active or has no second factor, and the code field is named `otp_token`, which Axes masks.
  * Setup (`/profile/two-factor/setup/`): requires the current password again; creates an unconfirmed secret (160 bits) and shows the QR code and the secret for manual entry; the factor is turned on only after a correct code from the app. The QR code is served as an SVG image of its own (`/profile/two-factor/setup/qr.svg`, `no-store`, only for the logged-in account's unconfirmed secret), because the policy allows images only from the own origin and no `data:` images (D-028, D-037). Ten recovery codes (40 bits each) are created and shown once. The session that confirmed the app counts as completed and its ID is renewed. A staff user or system administrator without a second factor can do this in the restricted session.
  * Re-authentication: password and, if a second factor is set up, a code, both under the abuse protection, in the forms for new recovery codes and for turning the second factor off. New recovery codes make the previous ones invalid. Users can turn their second factor off; staff users and system administrators cannot (the function refuses it as well).
  * Notifications (no link, `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 3): four new editable emails (D-039): second factor set up, removed, recovery codes renewed, recovery code used; queued like the password change notification and kept 24 hours if not processed.
  * Logging: until the security log of D-018 exists, setup, removal, renewal and use of a recovery code are written to the logger `leg_platform.security` with the internal account number and nothing else.
  * Budget of wrong codes (project owner, 2026-10-13: best practice and customer friendliness, a small residual risk is accepted; designed with the `security-reviewer`): `AuthenticatorDevice` counts the wrong codes of the account in a window of 24 hours from the first one (`failed_codes`, `failed_codes_since`; no new table; it disappears with the factor). Only a wrong code that reaches the check counts (code step, re-authentication forms, wrong recovery codes), under the account lock; attempts that Axes or the pause refuse and wrong passwords do not count, so that someone who knows only the email address can neither pause an account nor trigger the email. At 10 the owner gets one email without a link per window (the fifth notification, queued in the transaction; it does not use the request budgets of D-038) and one log line. At 15 code entry is paused, also for a correct code, until the window ends: the response is the lockout page with a text about the pause and the link to the password reset; like an Axes refusal it records nothing, so the Axes lockout is not extended, and the IP attempt is kept. A completed password reset ends the pause and leaves at most 10, never more attempts than before, and only once per window (`code_budget_relieved`; a mailbox thief gets at most the 5 attempts that the Axes reset gives, once per window). A successful login does not clear the budget: NIST SP 800-63B-4, section 3.2.2 says failures should be disregarded after a success, but then the budget of an attacker would grow with every login of the owner; deviation accepted. Escalating cool-off times were rejected: they would hang on the shared Axes counter and let someone who knows only the email address lock an account for a long time (D-017). NIST SP 800-63B-4, section 3.2.2 allows at most 100 consecutive failures per authenticator, this is far stricter; the estimate of the reviewer for the former limit (about 175,000 guesses per year) shrinks to at most 15 per account and day (about 5,500 per year, about 1.6 % chance of one right code per year in the extreme case, own arithmetic with three valid codes in 10^6, not checked against a source).
  * The pending login stores the session hash of the password and ends when the password changes. `cleanup_login_ip_attempts` also deletes abandoned unconfirmed setups and expired Axes attempts, and takes the IP buckets one by one with `skip_locked`, so that it cannot deadlock with a request (the order of locks is the same).
  * The devices are not in the administration, so that no secret can be shown and no staff user can add a device to an account.
* Verified facts (installed django-otp 1.7.4, django-axes 8.3.1, Django 6.1.2, segno 1.6.6; source read on 2026-10-13):
  * `OTPMiddleware` installs `user.is_verified()` from the device stored in the session (`otp_device_id`); `django_otp.login()` stores it, and the `user_logged_in` receiver calls it for a user object with an `otp_device` attribute after Django's `login()` renewed the session ID.
  * `AuthenticatorDevice.verify_token()` (written after django-otp's `TOTPDevice`) rejects a code at or below `last_t`; `RecoveryCodeDevice.verify_token()` deletes the used code with one statement, so that of two parallel requests only one succeeds. Each code attempt runs under a lock on the account row together with the lockout check and the record of a failure, so that parallel requests cannot exceed the limit.
  * `AxesStandaloneBackend.authenticate()` only checks the lockout and raises `PermissionDenied`; Django's `authenticate()` sends `user_login_failed` and Axes' middleware turns `request.axes_locked_out` into the lockout page.
  * Tests (`test_second_factor.py` and others; the full suite of 654 tests passes with Django 6.1.2 and with the minimum Django 6.0.9 (versions of `requirements/minimum-constraints.txt`), Python 3.14, MariaDB 12.3, deprecation warnings as errors; `check --deploy` was not run for this change): password step alone does not log in; pending login expiry, blocked account and removed factor; code reuse; recovery code use once and logging; session ID renewal; redirect targets; IP slot release and retention; lockout by wrong codes (account and IP); masking in Axes data; CSRF; restricted staff session, ended session, promotion in an open session, administration without the middleware, administration login redirect, devices not in the administration; setup, QR code, confirmation, recovery codes shown once, notifications; re-authentication under the abuse protection; removal rules; password change (current password, policy, other sessions end, notification, abuse protection, masking); every new page against the content security policy and the language rules.
* Not implemented (README "Current Status"): reset of a lost second factor by a system administrator and the emergency management command (D-004, D-005); the forced password change and setup at the first login of the initial administrator (D-005); idle and absolute session timeouts; ending other sessions when a user turns the second factor on or off; the security log storage and view (D-018); re-authentication for email change and account deletion (those functions do not exist yet).
* Security implications and open points:
  * The recovery codes are 40 bits; every attempt is counted by the abuse protection (five failures per 15 minutes per account and per IP address).
  * Until the Microsoft Graph delivery exists, the notifications are not sent in stage or production (deployment gate 1).
  * The administration stays restricted to `APP_ENV=local` (D-004; superseded by D-052).
* Decided by the project owner on 2026-10-13: the security values are stored encrypted or as digests (above); the device throttling follows best practice (see M-1); a mandatory second factor is enforced at the first login (the session of such an account has no privileges until it is set up) and the account is notified by email when it is set up, which is accepted as sufficient (M-3); the pending login of 5 minutes and ten recovery codes are accepted as common practice (not verified against a source; GitHub and Google issue 8 to 10 codes, which is recalled and not verified).
* Security review of 2026-10-13 (agent `security-reviewer`, read only, started by the main session; no critical or high finding) and how it was handled:
  * M-2 (fixed): the setup is bound to the session that started it (device ID in the session) and an unconfirmed secret is usable for 15 minutes only; tests for a second session, an old secret and another account's secret.
  * L-1, L-2, L-4, L-5, L-6 (fixed): the password change is logged and removes outstanding reset links; `csrfmiddlewaretoken` is masked by the abuse protection; notification jobs are queued inside the transaction; a refused change (race) leads back to the overview instead of an error, and `disable()` decides on the locked row. Test added that a password reset neither removes nor bypasses the second factor (`docs/SECURITY.md`, section 11).
  * M-1 (fixed, project owner: best practice): django-otp's device throttling (delay 2^(n-1) seconds, also for a correct code) let someone who knows the password keep the owner from using the code step by timed wrong attempts (a denial of service, not a bypass). The own device models have no throttling; the limit is the Axes account and IP limit (temporary, D-017), applied under the account lock. Test: failures do not lock the device itself.
  * M-3 (accepted by the project owner): a mandatory second factor can be set up in the restricted session by anyone who knows the password of a newly promoted account or of the initial administrator, before the real owner does. The session has no privileges until the factor is set up, and the owner is notified by email (not delivered before Graph delivery exists); the reset function (not implemented) is the recovery. Options not taken: an enrolment link by email or a one-time code from an administrator.
  * M-4 (fixed, project owner): secrets are encrypted or stored as digests (above); tests check that neither the key nor a code appears in the stored rows. L-3 (open): other sessions are not ended when the second factor is turned on. L-7 (decided by the project owner, 2026-10-13: the password change does not ask for the second factor; only the current password). L-8 (fixed, project owner: yes): the account is notified by email without a link when a recovery code is used (a fourth editable notification, queued in the transaction that consumes the code); parallel use of one code (app or recovery code) succeeds once, tested with threads on MariaDB. L-9 (fixed): the recovery code column has the binary collation.
  * Not tested: Django 6.0.9 (see the status below). The corrected version was reviewed again, see "Second review".
* Second review (agent `security-reviewer`, read only, started by the main session, after the own device models and the encryption; no critical or high finding) and how it was handled:
  * Fixed: M-1 recovery codes also match under the keys of `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS`; M-3 the password check of the password forms runs under the account lock like the code check (the login form itself keeps the check-then-act of D-017); M-4 tests with threads show that parallel wrong codes and wrong passwords from different addresses record at most five failures and end in the lockout; L-2 a code must be exactly six ASCII digits; L-3 the database refuses a second device per account; L-7 renewing the recovery codes keeps the device row, so that a session verified by a recovery code stays verified; L-8 the key is hidden from error reports; tests for encryption, key rotation, account deletion and code windows.
  * Handled after the consultation with the reviewer (2026-10-13): M-2 by the budget of wrong codes above (tests: notice once per window, no mail for password lockouts, pause even for a correct code, no record by Axes, window end, relief after a reset, parallel limit, German text without "ß"); L-4 and L-1 by the cleanup command (tested, also with a bucket held by a thread); L-6 by the session hash in the pending login. L-5 is documented in `docs/INSTALLATION.md` ("Changing `SECRET_KEY`") and the command that encrypts all keys again is planned with the emergency command (D-005), because a warning in the log already exists when a key cannot be decrypted.
  * Open: L-5 a replaced `SECRET_KEY` without fallback makes the second factors unusable until a system administrator resets them (reset not implemented); orphaned tables of the django-otp plugin apps, if an earlier development database has them, are not dropped; the cleanup commands are not scheduled yet (deployment gate 5).
* Third review (agent `security-reviewer`, read only, of the budget of wrong codes and the cleanup; no critical or high finding): fixed: a pending login without the stored hash ends without an error; `verify_token` saves only its own fields, so it cannot overwrite the budget; the pause is logged; the relief by a password reset is available once per window; tests for the recovery code and the forms during the pause, a second reset, a real reset between the two steps, and parallel wrong codes (exactly one email at the notice, never past the pause). Accepted: anyone who knows only the password can pause code entry for 24 hours (the owner ends it with a password reset); a wrong format or a replayed code counts as a wrong code; `delete_abandoned_enrolments` does not lock the account (a single `DELETE` with the filter; the filter is checked again by the database). Not reviewed: the django-axes source by the reviewer (checked by the tests), the Swiss German texts.\n* Sources: installed sources of django-otp 1.7.4 (`django_otp/__init__.py`, `middleware.py`, `models.py`, `plugins/otp_totp/models.py`, `plugins/otp_static/models.py`), django-axes 8.3.1 (`backends.py`, `middleware.py`, `handlers/database.py`) and Django 6.1.2 (`contrib/auth/__init__.py`, `contrib/admin/apps.py`, `sites.py`); https://django-otp-official.readthedocs.io/en/stable/overview.html (cited in D-004); OWASP Authentication Cheat Sheet and Multifactor Authentication Cheat Sheet (cited in D-004); D-004, D-016, D-017, D-028, D-038, D-039.

## D-041: Security Log, Session Timeouts, Reset of a Lost Second Factor, Initial Administrator and Scheduled Tasks

* Date: 2026-10-13
* Status: Decided (project owner, 2026-10-13: timeouts, link lifetime, log protection, wording of the two new emails and the typed confirmation of the emergency command); implemented and tested, not yet reviewed by the `security-reviewer`
* Context: README "Current Status": the open items of D-004, D-005 and D-018 and of the second-factor reviews (D-040): reset of a lost second factor, emergency command, forced password change and setup at the first login of the initial administrator, session timeouts, the persistent security log, the scheduling of the cleanup commands.
* Decision:
  * Security log (D-018, `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 10): the table `SecurityEvent` holds the time, the event (a fixed list), the internal number of the account concerned and of the account that acted, and whether the entry is kept for the long period. No other personal data, no email address, no free text. The numbers are not foreign keys: entries outlive the accounts and then refer to nobody (D-036). It is append-only in the application: the model and its queryset refuse updates, deletions and bulk creation; the administration shows it read only to system administrators (not by permission, as for the role administration) and offers no add, change, delete or action. Entries are written in the transaction of the change they record. They are also written to the logger `leg_platform.security`. Retention is set in the environment (`SECURITY_LOG_RETENTION_DAYS`, `SECURITY_LOG_LONG_RETENTION_DAYS`) with the values of D-018 (30 days, 365 days) as defaults and minimum: a lower value prevents the start. `cleanup_security_log` deletes expired entries. Events written so far: password change and reset, second factor set up, removed, reset (also the request for the link), recovery codes renewed and used, many wrong codes and the pause of code entry, lockout of an account after failed logins (once, when the limit is reached), changes of the access to the administration (`is_staff`, `is_superuser`) and of roles or permissions made in the administration (for roles and the permissions of a role without an account), the creation of the initial administrator and every use of the emergency command. Kept for the long period: the changes of rights and roles, the initial administrator, the emergency commands, and the reset of the second factor of staff users and system administrators. Not yet written, because the functions do not exist yet: blocking, unblocking, deletion, deactivation and reactivation, changes of the password or email address of another account by an administrator, the Microsoft Graph configuration. The individual failed logins stay in the records of the abuse protection package (30 days, `cleanup_login_ip_attempts`); their read-only view is still open (D-017).
  * Session timeouts (`docs/SECURITY.md`, section 3): enforced on the server by `SessionTimeoutMiddleware`. The session stores its start and its last use (written at most once a minute). Users: 30 minutes idle, 8 hours absolute. Staff users and system administrators (the accounts that must use a second factor): 15 minutes idle, 4 hours absolute. After a timeout the session is ended, the person sees the login page with a message and, after a GET request, returns to the page afterwards; a POST request changes nothing. A logged-in session without the stored times is ended (fails closed). The cookie and the stored session live as long as the longest absolute timeout (`SESSION_COOKIE_AGE`), so that the flows of the registration and password reset links, which keep their token in the session, are not cut off by the idle timeout of logged-in sessions; the expired stored sessions are removed by `clearsessions`.
  * Ending all sessions of an account: the session hash of an account covers the password and a counter (`User.session_version`, `User.end_all_sessions()`); raising the counter ends every session of the account, as a password change does. The session that made the change takes the new hash. It is used by the reset of the second factor. Ending the other sessions when a user turns the second factor on or off (open item of D-040) is not done yet; the mechanism exists.
  * Reset of a lost second factor (D-004): see `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 3, and `second_factor_reset.py`. In the administration, the account page of another account has the action "Reset two-factor authentication" (system administrators only, 403 for everybody else, also for the own account). A user (neither staff user nor system administrator): the administrator requests the removal; the worker creates a single-use token (24 hours, stored as keyed digest like the other tokens, never in the job) and sends the link; opening the link only stores the token in the session and leaves the URL (as for the password reset); the owner confirms on a page with a button; then the devices are deleted, all sessions end, the owner is notified and the event is logged. A newer link replaces the older one; a link stops working if the account is no longer active, became a staff user or has no second factor. A staff user or system administrator: reset at once after the administrator ticked that the identity was verified in person or by calling back a number known independently of the account; the person sets the factor up again at the next login (D-040, M-3 accepted). Two new editable emails (D-039): the link and the notification of a reset. Rules are checked again in `second_factor_reset.py`, in the transaction, with the account rows locked in the order of their primary key (no deadlock between two administrators).
  * Initial administrator (D-005): `manage.py setup_initial_administrator` reads the address from `BOOTSTRAP_SYSTEM_ADMIN_EMAIL`, asks for the password twice with `getpass` (it refuses to run without a terminal, so the password cannot come from a pipe or a file), refuses while any account with `is_superuser` exists and uses the existing, locked `create_superuser()`. The account gets `password_change_required`; `SecondFactorMiddleware` then lets the session only change the password and log out, and the administration is closed for it (also in `PortalAdminSite.has_permission()`). The new password must differ from the old one; the password change and the password reset by link clear the flag. After that, the existing rule applies: the second factor must be set up before anything else. Django's `createsuperuser` stays disabled and points to the new command.
  * Emergency command `manage.py emergency_access` (D-005): `reset-second-factor N` (any account, also the last system administrator), `unblock-account N` (a blocked account), `reencrypt-second-factor-keys` (the command planned in D-040, L-5). It asks for confirmation (`--no-input` skips it), prints the internal number only, and logs every use for the long period.
  * Scheduled tasks: `manage.py run_scheduled_tasks` runs `delete_expired_registrations`, `cleanup_mail_requests`, `cleanup_login_ip_attempts`, `cleanup_security_log` and `clearsessions` one after the other; a failing task does not stop the others and makes the command fail. A test checks that every `cleanup_*` and `delete_expired_*` command is included. The scheduler itself (cron, systemd timer or other) is decided with the hosting (D-008); the installation guide says what to schedule.
* Verified facts (source read on 2026-10-13):
  * Tests: `test_security_log.py`, `test_session_timeout.py`, `test_second_factor_reset.py`, `test_scheduled_tasks.py` and additions to the environment, log filter and catalogue tests; the whole suite (725 tests) passes with Django 6.1.2, Python 3.14, MariaDB 12.3 and deprecation warnings as errors. Not run: Django 6.0.9, `check --deploy`, CI.
  * OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet, "Session Expiration": "Common idle timeouts ranges are 2-5 minutes for high-value applications and 15-30 minutes for low risk applications"; "If the application is intended to be used by an office worker for a full day, an appropriate absolute timeout range could be between 4 and 8 hours"; timeouts "must be enforced server-side". The values above are within these ranges; the high-value range (2-5 minutes) was not chosen because it would make the administration hard to use; the shorter privileged values are the compromise.
  * Django 6.1.2 `AbstractBaseUser.get_session_auth_hash()` is an HMAC of the password field (`_get_session_auth_hash(secret)`), and `get_user()` compares it with the session; `get_session_auth_fallback_hash()` yields the same hash under the keys of `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS`. The project overrides `_get_session_auth_hash()`, so both use the counter.
  * django-axes 8.3.1 (`handlers/database.py`) sends `user_locked_out` also for refused attempts during a lockout; the project records the lockout only when the failure that reaches the limit is counted.
  * MariaDB cannot group by day without time zone tables (`ValueError: Database returned an invalid datetime value`), so the administration of the security log has no date hierarchy.
* Security implications and open points:
  * The security log is append-only only within the application; persons with direct database access can change it (as for the retention flag, D-016). Decided by the project owner (2026-10-13): not pursued further, because whoever can log in to the server can do everything anyway; no stricter split of database accounts for the log.
  * A link for the removal of the second factor gives the second factor away to anybody who controls the mailbox and gets the administrator to start it. This is the rule of D-004 (the confirmation through the registered mailbox); an administrator should not start it on a request that arrives through an unverified channel. The password is still needed to log in afterwards.
  * Sessions of other devices stay valid when a user sets the second factor up or turns it off (D-040, L-3).
  * Reset by the administrator for staff users relies on the administrator's confirmation; the application cannot check it. The confirmation is not stored beyond the log entry of the reset with the account of the administrator.
* Decided by the project owner (2026-10-13): the timeouts (users 30 minutes and 8 hours, staff users and administrators 15 minutes and 4 hours) and the lifetime of the removal link (24 hours) follow best practice as above; no further protection of the log table against persons with server access.
* Confirmed by the project owner (2026-10-13): the wording of the two new emails (the link to remove two-factor authentication and the notification of a reset) and that the emergency command `emergency_access` asks for a typed confirmation (`--no-input` skips it).
* Review of 2026-10-13 (agents `security-reviewer` and `designer`, read only, started by the main session; no critical or high security finding) and how it was handled:
  * Fixed: M-1 the user administration wrote back the form's old password hash, session counter and password-change flag (they are now taken from the locked row; test with a stale instance); L-1 the session-timeout redirect never puts the token of a link into the login URL; L-6 changing the access to the administration or the roles of an account ends all its sessions (tested); L-4 (partly) deleting a role is logged; L-10 the setup command refuses a terminal that cannot hide the input.
  * Open, accepted or to be decided: L-2 the reset job names the account by its address, so after a change of address another holder could receive a link (they must still confirm it themselves); L-3 some log entries (initial administrator, lockout, re-encryption) are written after the change, not in its transaction, so the sentence "written in the transaction of the change" holds only where the code says so; L-4 (rest) the role entry names no role and is written on every save; L-5 `set_privileges()` and `set_access_relations()` do not log, only the administration does (the design account seed is the only other caller); L-7 individual failed logins stay in the Axes records (about 15 minutes), only the lockout is in the log, so the 30 days of D-018 are not met for them; L-8 lockouts can fill the log (bounded per IP); L-9 no limit on how often an administrator can send the link; an expired initial password and the removal of old reset tokens when an account is promoted. Not reviewed: django-axes and django-otp sources, the MariaDB lock order (no test of two parallel resets), email delivery, German texts.
  * Design (read from the running application, desktop and 390 px): corrected: the breadcrumb of the three custom administration pages, the label of the retention filter of the log, one warning instead of two in the forced password change (and no link to the profile there), the wording of the session message and of the two link pages. Open: UX-001 the text contrast of the message boxes (info 2.8:1, success 2.6:1, warning 2.0:1, DESIGN.md asks for 4.5:1; needs a decision on DESIGN.md section 3.6), UX-007 the administration page of the reset should name the account and list the consequences, UX-008 the account page should show whether two-factor authentication is on, the reset action as destructive (DESIGN.md section 6), the target size of secondary links, no dark mode switch, no favicon, empty navigation without privacy and website URL, no contact route for a locked-out person if no website is configured.
  * Tests after the corrections: the affected modules pass (169 tests); the whole suite (725 tests before the corrections) was not run again, and Django 6.0.9, `check --deploy` and CI were not run. Nothing is open for this decision except the review by the `security-reviewer`.
* Sources: https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Session_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html (checked 2026-10-13); installed sources of Django 6.1.2 (`django/contrib/auth/base_user.py`, `__init__.py`) and django-axes 8.3.1; D-004, D-005, D-012, D-016, D-017, D-018, D-036, D-037, D-038, D-039, D-040.

## D-042: Rights Only With a Second Factor, Only the Newest Password Reset Link, Recovery Codes Unique per Account

* Date: 2026-10-13
* Status: Decided (project owner, 2026-10-13: fix the findings L-1 to L-3 of the review before the push and the failure of the CI job "latest"); implemented and tested, not yet reviewed again by the `security-reviewer`
* Context: the review of D-040 and D-041 before the push (agent `security-reviewer`, read only, started by the main session; no critical, high or medium finding) reported three low findings, and the CI job "latest" (Python 3.14, Django 6.1.2, MariaDB 12.3) failed on a deprecation warning of Django 6.1 (run 38466208248; the job "minimum" passed).
* Decision:
  * L-3, rights only with a second factor: the administration grants `is_staff` or `is_superuser` only to an account that already has a confirmed authenticator app (`second_factor.has_second_factor()`; an unconfirmed app or recovery codes alone do not count). Otherwise whoever knows the password of the account could set up their own authenticator app at the next login and then use the rights (D-040, M-3). The form of the account refuses the change with a translated explanation; `save_model()` checks the rule again on the locked account row (the person could turn the second factor off in between; `totp.disable()` locks the same row) and refuses with 403. Removing rights is always possible. Groups and direct permissions are not restricted: without `is_staff` they open nothing today, because only the administration checks permissions (`PortalAdminSite.has_permission()` requires `is_staff`). The initial system administrator (`setup_initial_administrator`, D-005) is created without a second factor and must set it up at the first login (D-041); a staff user or system administrator whose second factor was reset sets it up again at the next login. These are the only remaining cases of M-3. The rule proves that a confirmed authenticator app exists, not that the owner controls it: someone who knew the password earlier could have set up their own app (the owner then received the email that the second factor was set up). Before granting rights, the administrator therefore confirms with the person that they set up the second factor themselves (`docs/SECURITY.md`, section 4). `seed_design_accounts` (local only) creates the authenticator app before it grants the rights; `emergency_access` grants no rights. The rule is enforced in the administration, the only function that grants rights, not in `User.set_privileges()`.
  * L-1, only the newest password reset link: a new request below the per-address limit deletes the earlier reset links of the account before the new one is created, as the link for removing the second factor already does (D-041). A request above the limit is not processed (`mail_queue._claim()` deletes a suppressed job without calling the handler), so the links already sent stay valid then (`docs/SECURITY.md`, section 3). A session that opened an earlier link can no longer set a password, because the page looks the token up again.
  * L-2, recovery codes unique per account: the digest of a recovery code is unique per device (`accounts_recovery_code_unique_per_device`, migration `0009_recovery_code_unique_per_device`) instead of across the instance. A code is only ever checked against the codes of the account's own device (`RecoveryCodeDevice.verify_token()` filters `self.codes`), and the single use by one `DELETE` statement is unchanged. Before, a random match of a new 40-bit code with a code of another account would have made the setup or the renewal fail with a database error.
  * Django 6.1: overriding `ModelAdmin.get_actions()` without the parameter `action_location` is deprecated (`RemovedInDjango2028Warning`). The administration of the security log no longer overrides it; it sets `actions = None`, the documented way to disable all actions of a model, which works the same on Django 6.0 and 6.1. It was the only override of `get_actions()` or `get_action_choices()` in the project.
* Verified facts:
  * Installed Django 6.1.2, `django/contrib/admin/options.py`: `_get_actions_with_action_location()` warns when `get_actions` has no parameter `action_location`; `get_actions()` returns `{}` when `self.actions is None`. Installed Django 6.0.9 (minimum): `get_actions(self, request)` returns `{}` when `self.actions is None`, and `ActionLocation` does not exist.
  * Tests: `test_admin.py` (no rights without a confirmed app, also for an existing staff user who is to become a system administrator; an unconfirmed app and recovery codes do not count; removal of rights; a second factor removed between the form check and the save; German message), `test_password_reset.py` (the earlier link is invalid and the new one works; a session with the earlier link cannot set a password; the last link stays valid above the limit), `test_second_factor.py` (a code that another account also has does not break the renewal, and each account uses only its own code; a device cannot hold the same code twice), `test_security_log.py` (no actions on the list and the change page).
* Security implications:
  * A third party who knows only an email address can, within the per-address limit, make a reset link that the owner has not used yet invalid by requesting a new one; the new link goes to the same mailbox. This is the accepted behaviour of the registration and the link for removing the second factor.
  * M-3 of D-040 remains for the initial system administrator and after a reset of the second factor of a staff user or system administrator.
  * Accepted consequence (re-review, Low-1; availability only): the worker deletes the earlier reset links and creates the new one in a transaction that is committed before the email is sent. If sending fails and the job is repeated (up to `MAX_ATTEMPTS`), every attempt replaces the link again, so during an outage of the email delivery the owner can be left without a working link until a new request is delivered.
  * If roles with permissions are later used outside the administration (for authenticated users without `is_staff`), whether such roles need a second factor must be decided again.
  * A password reset keeps `is_staff`, `is_superuser`, the groups and the direct permissions of the account and removes no privilege: `complete_reset()` changes only the password, the reset links and the temporary lockout of the account (verified by reading the code, 2026-10-14; see D-043).
* Open (to be decided by the project owner; recommended by the `security-reviewer` in the re-review; decided in D-043, which keeps the rule in the administration only): enforce the L-3 rule also centrally in `User._check_privilege_grant()` on the update path, as defence in depth, before any second function that grants rights is added.
* Alternatives: enforcing the rule in `User.set_privileges()` (would also cover future callers, but every caller, including the tests, would have to create a second factor first, and the initial administrator path would need an exception); an enrolment link by email or a one-time code from an administrator (D-040, M-3; not taken).
* Sources: installed sources of Django 6.1.2 and 6.0.9 (`django/contrib/admin/options.py`); https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/ref/contrib/admin/actions/ ("Disabling all actions for a particular ModelAdmin", checked on 2026-10-13); D-004, D-005, D-012, D-038, D-040, D-041.

## D-043: Rights Rule Stays in the Administration; Locks and Purpose Check for Password Reset

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided (project owner, 2026-10-14: the rule of D-042 for granting rights is not enforced centrally in the account logic; the findings N-1, N-2 and I-1 of the review are fixed). Not yet reviewed again by the `security-reviewer`.
* Context: the review of commit c0678b5 by the `security-reviewer` (read only) reported M-1 (the rule of D-042, L-3, is enforced only in the administration, not centrally), N-1 (parallel password reset requests could leave more than one valid reset link), N-2 (the docstring of `totp.verify_code()` claimed a device lock that does not exist), I-1 (a reset link of another purpose could set a password) and N-3 (the current password is checked in form validation outside the lock of the password change).
* Decision:
  * M-1: the second-factor rule for granting `is_staff` and `is_superuser` (D-042, L-3) stays in the administration (`UserAdminForm._clean_privileges`, `UserAdmin.save_model`). It is not added to `User._check_privilege_grant()` or `User.set_privileges()`; the open point of D-042 is decided this way. Residual risk: code outside the administration that grants `is_staff` or `is_superuser` must implement the same check and carry a test. Every review that adds such code checks this.
  * N-1: `process_reset_request()` locks the account row (`select_for_update()`) and checks the status under the lock before it replaces the reset links, in one transaction. Parallel requests replace the links one after another, so only the last link stays valid. An unknown address still returns `None` without a lock on any row.
  * N-2: the docstring of `totp.verify_code()` states its precondition: the caller holds the row lock of the account, as `second_factor.verify_second_factor()` does (D-040). No runtime check was added. The parallel test for codes of the app takes the same lock; the parallel test for recovery codes is unchanged, because a recovery code is single use by one `DELETE` statement.
  * I-1: `complete_reset()` accepts only tokens of the purpose `PASSWORD_RESET`. A registration or two-factor reset link cannot set a password.
* Verified facts (by reading the code, 2026-10-14):
  * `second_factor.verify_second_factor()` is the only production caller of `totp.verify_code()` and takes the account row lock first.
  * `AuthenticatorDevice.verify_token()` reads and saves `last_t` without a lock of its own; `RecoveryCodeDevice.verify_token()` removes the code with one `DELETE`.
  * `PasswordChangeView.form_valid()` locks the account row; the current password is checked earlier, in `ChangePasswordForm.clean_current_password()`.
* Security implications:
  * N-3, open and not fixed: the current password is checked before the account row is locked, so two parallel password changes of the same account can both pass the check. The check is still a correct password check and failures are still counted (`authenticate()` sends `user_login_failed`, read by the abuse protection); the race concerns only two changes that both have the correct current password. For the project owner's decision whether the check moves under the lock.
  * The threaded tests of N-1 and of the parallel app code run only with MariaDB (tag `mariadb`); their results are recorded under Tests.
* Tests (2026-10-14, MariaDB, `manage.py`): `manage.py check` reported no issues; `manage.py makemigrations --check` reported no changes; `leg_platform.accounts.tests.test_password_reset` and `leg_platform.accounts.tests.test_second_factor` ran 177 tests, OK (includes the new tests for N-1, I-1 and the lock of the app code). The full suite has not been run after these changes.
* Alternatives: enforcing the rule in `User._check_privilege_grant()` as defence in depth (recommended by the `security-reviewer`; not taken by the owner's decision); enforcing it in `User.set_privileges()` (see D-042).
* Consequences: this decision does not change the Microsoft Graph delivery, which is committed separately (commit 88ee6ae). Its documentation under D-006 is still pending.
* Sources: D-012, D-040, D-042; Django documentation of `QuerySet.select_for_update()` (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/ref/models/querysets/#select-for-update), which requires a transaction; the code of this repository as read on 2026-10-14.

## D-044: Password Loss Path and Emergency Procedure

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided. The project owner decided the password-loss path on 2026-10-14 ("Forgot password" and set a new password). The other points are decided by the AI on the basis of the sources below and are open to review by the project owner.
* Context: the open points were to be decided by comparing how established portals solve them, not by asking. Lost passwords, lost second factors and blocked accounts were not yet described as one procedure.
* Decision:
  * Lost password: the person uses "Forgot password?" (login, lockout and invalid-link pages), opens the single-use link (valid 24 hours) and sets a new password, then logs in normally. No operator command is needed. The reset does not log the person in automatically.
  * The reset does not remove or change the second factor. After a reset, the second factor is still required. A lost second factor is handled separately: a system administrator resets it in the administration (for users, the reset is sent as a link to the owner; for staff users and system administrators it takes effect at once); if no other system administrator is available, the operator runs `emergency_access reset-second-factor N` on the server.
  * A blocked account receives no reset email (only active accounts do). It is unblocked by `emergency_access unblock-account N` on the server.
  * The procedure is written down in `docs/INSTALLATION.md` (section "Emergency commands").
  * Failed-attempt records: no deviation from D-018. D-018 and `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 10, already set 30 days for the failed-attempt records of the abuse protection. The note of a possible deviation from an earlier review is withdrawn.
* Verified facts:
  * OWASP Forgot Password Cheat Sheet (https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Forgot_Password_Cheat_Sheet.html, fetched 2026-10-14): consistent response for existing and non-existing accounts; single-use tokens that expire and are linked to one user; no change to the account before a valid token is presented; rate limit per account; after recovery, existing sessions and outstanding reset links are invalidated; the user logs in through the usual mechanism and is not logged in automatically; the user is notified by email. A password reset alone may not restore control after a suspected compromise; MFA reset is covered by the OWASP Multifactor Authentication Cheat Sheet (section \"Resetting MFA\"; not yet fetched, therefore unverified).
  * Code of this repository (read 2026-10-14): `process_reset_request()` sends only to active accounts and replaces the reset links under a row lock; `complete_reset()` changes the password, deletes the reset links, ends the lockout and records `PASSWORD_RESET`; it does not call `login()`; `notify_password_changed()` sends the notification; the admin resets the second factor directly for staff and system administrators and by link for other users (`reset_privileged()` and `request_link()` in `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`).
  * Full test suite after the test fix in `leg_platform/tests/test_mail_graph.py` (2026-10-14): 774 tests, OK.
* Consequences: a person who lost both password and second factor needs a system administrator or the operator; the second factor is never removed by a password reset, so an attacker with access to the mailbox alone cannot take over a protected account. This is the intended protection, and the OWASP compromise guidance is followed by keeping the second factor.
* Open, not decided by the AI: the operator identity in the emergency log (who ran the command). Recording it adds personal data to the security log; the owner decides whether and how (D-018, D-041).
* Sources: OWASP Forgot Password Cheat Sheet (see above); D-004, D-005, D-018, D-040, D-041, D-042, D-043.

## D-045: Language of Expected Error Messages in Graph Certificate Tests

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided by the AI (test-only change, no product behaviour changed). Open to review by the project owner.
* Context: four tests in `leg_platform/tests/test_mail_graph.py` (`GraphConfigurationTests`) expected English substrings from the certificate checks in `GraphConfiguration.check_certificate()` (`leg_platform/accounts/models.py`). The active language in the test run is `de-ch`, so the raised `ValidationError` messages are Swiss German and the assertions failed.
* Decision:
  * The four assertions compare against the `de-ch` translation of the English message ID, produced by the helper `german()` in `leg_platform/tests/test_mail_graph.py`. The helper uses `translation.override("de-ch")` and `gettext()`.
  * The `de-ch` texts are not changed. The English message IDs in the code are not changed.
  * The Graph certificate checks, including the minimum key size of 2048 bits and the expiry check, are not changed.
  * The same mechanism is already used in `german_message()` in `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_admin.py`.
* Verified facts:
  * Django translation documentation (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/topics/i18n/translation/, fetched 2026-10-14): `gettext()` returns the translation for the active language; `translation.override()` temporarily activates a language and restores the previous one on exit.
  * Message IDs in `leg_platform/accounts/models.py` (about lines 1291–1305) and their `de-ch` msgstrs in `locale/de_CH/LC_MESSAGES/django.po` (about lines 1283–1293) were read in this session.
  * `manage.py check` and `makemigrations --check --dry-run` passed in this session. The four tests were not run in this session; the test run is the tester's task.
* Rationale: the users see `de-ch` messages (AGENTS.md, section 2.4), so the tests should check the text the user sees. Changing the messages to English would break the Swiss German user interface and would be a product change.
* Security implications: none. The test change does not weaken any check; the same validation errors are still expected and the same conditions are still tested.
* Alternatives: compare against English substrings with the active language forced to English in the test (rejected: it would test a text the user does not see); compare error codes instead of messages (possible later, but this is a larger change and is not needed now).
* Sources (official sources or verification steps): Django documentation "Translation" (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/topics/i18n/translation/); repository precedent `german_message()` in `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_admin.py`; `leg_platform/accounts/models.py` and `locale/de_CH/LC_MESSAGES/django.po` as read in this session.
* Consequences: the four Graph tests depend on the `de-ch` translations in `django.po`. If a translation is changed, the test helper follows it automatically. The statement in D-044 about a full suite run (774 tests, OK) was not verified in this session and stays open until the tester confirms it.

## D-046: Sequential Test Runs

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided by the AI (process rule, no product behaviour changed). Open to review by the project owner.
* Context: the test database name is fixed as `test_<NAME>`. Two test runs on the same MariaDB server overlap and then fail in the test setup with `IntegrityError` 1062 (duplicate entry), for example for `admin@example.org`. This was reported in the session; it was not reproduced in this step.
* Decision: only one test process runs at a time against the same database server. No settings change for a per-run test database name in Phase 1.
* Verified facts:
  * Installed Django source `.venv/Lib/site-packages/django/db/backends/base/creation.py` (read 2026-10-14): `TEST_DATABASE_PREFIX = "test_"` (line 16); `_get_test_db_name()` returns `test_` plus the database `NAME` unless `TEST["NAME"]` is set (lines 203-214); `_create_test_db()` drops and recreates an existing test database when `autoclobber` is true, without a prompt (lines 237-254).
  * Installed Django source `.venv/Lib/site-packages/django/test/utils.py` (read 2026-10-14): `create_test_db()` is called with `autoclobber=not interactive` (line 222), so `manage.py test --noinput` drops an existing test database.
* Rationale: a second run drops the database the first run still uses, which corrupts the first run and makes its results unreliable.
* Security implications: none for the product. The test database is named `test_<NAME>` and is dropped by the test runner; production data must not be used in local tests (AGENTS.md, section 9).
* Alternatives: a per-run database name through `TEST["NAME"]` in the settings (not chosen for Phase 1; it changes the settings and needs its own review); a separate database server per run.
* Sources (official sources or verification steps): installed Django source files listed above (read 2026-10-14); AGENTS.md, section 9.
* Consequences: test runs are queued, not parallel. Open: parallel CI runs, which would need a per-run database name or a separate server.

## D-047: Model Registry Test Includes GraphConfiguration

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided by the AI (test-only change, no product behaviour changed). Open to review by the project owner.
* Context: the test `test_user_model_is_registered_with_issued_numbers_hidden` in `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_model_rules.py` (`UserModelConfigurationTests`) asserts the exact set of models registered in the Django admin. `accounts.GraphConfiguration` is registered on purpose, so the expected set did not match.
* Decision: `accounts.GraphConfiguration` is added to the expected set. The assertion stays an exact set comparison, so any other extra or missing model still fails it.
* Verified facts:
  * `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, line 1031: `admin.site.register(GraphConfiguration, GraphConfigurationAdmin)`.
  * `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, class `GraphConfigurationAdmin` (lines 825-831): only system administrators see and change it; no row can be added or deleted.
  * `docs/PROJECT.md`, section "Email" (around line 212): "The system administrator configures Microsoft Graph in a dedicated section of the Django admin". Line 220: "Configuration and credentials are separated between local, stage and production, and between instances."
  * `docs/PROJECT.md` does not contain the phrase "admin-managed configuration" as a literal heading; the passage above is the closest match.
* Rationale: the Microsoft Graph settings are instance configuration that the system administrator manages in the admin (D-006). Leaving the model out of the test would hide an intended admin registration; keeping it in the exact set keeps the test strict.
* Security implications: the registration is restricted to system administrators (`has_module_permission`, `has_view_permission`, `has_change_permission` return `request.user.is_superuser`; `has_add_permission` and `has_delete_permission` return `False`). The test checks only the registration, not these permissions.
* Alternatives: a separate test for the admin permissions of `GraphConfigurationAdmin` (not added in this step; it is a possible follow-up); excluding the model from the test (rejected: it would weaken the strict check).
* Sources (official sources or verification steps): `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py` (lines 825-831 and 1031) and `docs/PROJECT.md` (around lines 212-220), both read on 2026-10-14; D-006.
* Consequences: no test currently asserts the admin permissions of `GraphConfigurationAdmin` (search of the test folders on 2026-10-14). Open: a test for these permissions.

## D-048: Open Points of the Microsoft Graph Sender and Minimum Version of requests

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Partly decided by the AI on the instruction of the project owner. The points marked "open" are not decided. Open to review by the project owner.
* Context: the open points of the Microsoft Graph sender (D-006) and the minimum version of the `requests` dependency: the `Auto-Submitted` header (M2), the console-mode test send (M1), a rate limit for the test send (I2), the size limit of the certificate upload (I3), and the minimum version of `requests` (Task 1).
* Decision:
  * Minimum version of `requests` (decided 2026-10-14 on the binding instruction of the project owner): `requests>=2.34.2` is declared in `pyproject.toml` and `requests==2.34.2` is pinned in `requirements/minimum-constraints.txt`. Reason: `leg_platform/mail.py` imports `requests` directly, and 2.34.2 is the installed version and the newest release checked. Sources: the PyPI release of 2.34.2 dated 2026-05-14 (earlier check, not repeated on 2026-10-14); the installed version `.venv\Lib\site-packages\requests-2.34.2.dist-info` and `pip show requests` on 2026-10-14. Open: a lower minimum needs a verified support source (official release or support information showing that the older release line still receives security updates). Until then, no lower minimum is declared.
  * Code changes (2026-10-14): the review findings M1, M2 (warning kept), L1 to L4, I1, I4 and I5 are implemented in the code. I2 stays open, and I3 keeps the 16 KiB check.
  * `Auto-Submitted` (M2): the Graph backend keeps not sending this header and logs a warning. No `x-` header is added as a substitute. The mitigation stays: automatic replies of the sender mailbox are switched off (`docs/INSTALLATION.md`, step 6). Risk: an automatic reply of the sender mailbox may reach a user if this mitigation is not in place. Open for the project owner: whether the mailbox settings are the control, or an alternative sender path is needed.
  * Console mode (M1): the test send is refused in console mode, because no Graph request is made there. This is implemented and kept.
  * Review findings F1 to F5 and the console-mode test gap (2026-10-14, developer step). The changes are in the code and the documentation. No test was run in this step: the project owner excluded `manage.py test` (tester run in progress). Status per item:
    * F1, `docs/INSTALLATION.md` (line 123): the `x-` sentence quotes Microsoft Learn verbatim instead of paraphrasing it. Closed. Source: Microsoft Learn message resource page (see Verified facts). Closed by L-1 (2026-10-14): D-006 (line 104) now quotes the source verbatim; the earlier paraphrase is withdrawn.
    * F2, `leg_platform/accounts/models.py` (`check_certificate`, around line 1282) and `leg_platform/mail.py` (`_access_token`, `except UnicodeDecodeError`): a private key with non-ASCII characters is refused when it is set (F2a), and a stored non-ASCII key gives a generic `GraphEmailError` at send time without a token request (F2b). Closed in code; tests added, not run. Open: whether the PEM loader accepts non-ASCII text in front of the `-----BEGIN` line is not verified. The test `test_non_ascii_private_key_is_refused_and_not_stored` fails before the fix only if the loader accepts such text.
    * F3, `leg_platform/mail.py` (constructor, `except OSError`): an `OSError` (including `requests` exceptions) from the tenant discovery in `ConfidentialClientApplication(...)` is raised as "Microsoft did not answer the token request; no email was sent." without the cause (`from None`). Closed in code; test added, not run. Sources: see Verified facts (msal `application.py` 741-743, `authority.py` 98-101, `requests/exceptions.py` 20, 70, 82).
    * F4, `leg_platform/mail.py` (`_TokenSession.request`): the Graph timeout is set unconditionally instead of with `setdefault`, so an explicit `timeout=None` cannot remove it. Closed in code; test added, not run. Verified: `dict.setdefault` keeps an existing value, including `None`. Not verified: how `requests` treats `timeout=None`; the test checks only the value passed on.
    * F5, `leg_platform/settings.py` (`LOGGING`, logger `msal.oauth2cli.oauth2`): level CRITICAL, handler `console`, `propagate` False. Closed in code; verified by source reading, not executed. A WARNING level would not suppress the body log, because msal logs a non-JSON token response with `Logger.exception`, which logs at ERROR (see Verified facts).
    * Console-mode test gap: the send-test POST path was covered only by a GET test. Closed as a test: `GraphAdminTests.test_post_to_send_test_in_console_mode_is_refused_without_contacting_microsoft`. The code path (`_send_test`, from line 1004, returns before the backend call) was read, not run.
  * Test send rate limit (I2): open, not implemented.
  * Upload size of the certificate (I3): the 16 KiB check in the application stays. The web server or reverse proxy must limit the request body size; this is documented in `docs/INSTALLATION.md` (Microsoft Graph sender). The value of this limit is open.
  * L-1 (closed, docs): D-006 quotes the Microsoft Learn message resource verbatim instead of paraphrasing it; source: D-006 Sources, checked 2026-10-14.
  * L-2 (accepted residual risk, docs only): msal logs a signing failure at ERROR; likelihood low; owner may set the logger to CRITICAL; source: msal `oauth2cli/assertion.py` lines 125-130, see Security implications.
  * L-3 (closed in code): the constructor of `ConfidentialClientApplication` maps any other exception to the generic `GraphEmailError` without the cause; source: msal `authority.py` lines 98-101, 304-305, see Verified facts; test `test_other_error_in_constructor_is_raised_without_tenant_or_url`.
* Verified facts:
  * `requests`: the installed version is 2.34.2 (`.venv\Lib\site-packages\requests-2.34.2.dist-info`, listed 2026-10-14). The installed `msal` 1.39.0 declares `requests <3,>=2.0.0` (`.venv\Lib\site-packages\msal-1.39.0.dist-info\METADATA`, line 28, read 2026-10-14). The official `requests` pages checked on 2026-10-14 (https://github.com/psf/requests/blob/main/.github/SECURITY.md and https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/latest/community/support/) describe how to report vulnerabilities and how to get help. They state no list of supported release lines and no end-of-support dates. The release-process page was not checked. Therefore the support status of older releases is not verified.
  * `Auto-Submitted`: Microsoft Learn, message resource type (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/graph/api/resources/message?view=graph-rest-1.0, fetched 2026-10-14), section "This resource supports": "Adding your own data as custom Internet message headers. Add custom headers only when creating a message, and name them starting with "x-"." The page does not say how Graph treats other header names. Whether Graph accepts or refuses `Auto-Submitted` at runtime is not tested (as in D-006).
  * `leg_platform/mail.py`, lines 172-173 (read 2026-10-14): the backend logs a warning when `Auto-Submitted` is among the extra headers and does not send it.
  * Console mode: `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, `_test_refusal()` (from line 963): in console mode the refusal text is returned. Its Swiss German translation is in `locale/de_CH/LC_MESSAGES/django.po` (line 1349 and the following msgstr; read 2026-10-14).
  * Test send: `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, `send_test_view()` requires `has_change_permission()`, which is `request.user.is_superuser`. The test email goes only to the address of the requesting user (`request.user.email`). No rate limit or throttling was found in `send_test_view()` or `_send_test()` (read 2026-10-14).
  * Logging: `_send_test()` (from line 1004) calls `security_log.record(Event.GRAPH_TEST_EMAIL_REQUESTED, actor=request.user)` at line 1007, before the refusal check. Every attempt is logged, including refused ones (D-018).
  * Upload: `GRAPH_UPLOAD_MAX_BYTES = 16 * 1024` (`leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, line 777). `_read_upload()` (line 815) checks the size and runs in the form's `clean()`, that is after Django has received the form.
  * Django settings reference (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.0/ref/settings/, checked 2026-10-14): `DATA_UPLOAD_MAX_MEMORY_SIZE` (default 2.5 MB) is calculated without file upload data. Under ASGI the entire request may be spooled to disk before this limit is enforced, and Django recommends additional protections in front of Django that limit the entire request payload. `FILE_UPLOAD_MAX_MEMORY_SIZE` (default 2.5 MB) decides when an upload is streamed to the file system.
  * msal 1.39.0 (`.venv\Lib\site-packages\msal`, read 2026-10-14): `authority.py` line 75 (`__init__`), line 89 (`self._http_client = http_client`), lines 98-100 (`tenant_discovery(...)`), line 101 (`except ValueError:`); `application.py` line 704 (`self.http_client = http_client`), line 721 (`ThrottledHttpClient(`), lines 741-743 (`self.authority = Authority(` with `self.http_client,`). Tenant discovery therefore runs inside the `ConfidentialClientApplication` constructor. `oauth2cli/oauth2.py` line 164 (`self.logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)`) and line 302 (`self.logger.exception("Token response is not in json format: %s", resp.text)`): a non-JSON token response body is logged at ERROR level.
  * Python logging (`C:\Python314\Lib\logging\__init__.py`, read 2026-10-14): line 1553 (`Logger.error`, `if self.isEnabledFor(ERROR):`), line 1556 (`Logger.exception`), line 1560 (`Logger.exception` calls `self.error`), line 1730 (`callHandlers`), line 1774 (`isEnabledFor`).
  * requests (`.venv\Lib\site-packages\requests\exceptions.py`, read 2026-10-14): line 20 (`RequestException(IOError)`), line 70 (`ConnectionError`), line 82 (`Timeout`). Since Python 3, `IOError` is `OSError`, so these exceptions are `OSError` subclasses.
  * msal 1.39.0 (read 2026-10-14, `.venv\Lib\site-packages\msal`): `authority.py` lines 98-101 (`tenant_discovery` call, `except ValueError:` at 101), 114 (`raise ValueError(error_message)`), 116-117 (`openid_config[...]`, KeyError possible), 169 (`raise ValueError(`), 176 (`payload['tenant_discovery_endpoint']`, KeyError possible), 275 (`canonicalize` raises ValueError), 292-294 (`http_client.get`, `json.loads`), 298 (`raise ValueError` for HTTP 4xx), 304 (`resp.raise_for_status()`), 305 (`raise RuntimeError(`, fallback not caught by the code). `application.py` lines 741-748 (`Authority(`, `except OSError:`), 758 (bare `raise`), 759 (second `Authority(`). Result: OSError and ValueError were handled; KeyError and RuntimeError were not (L-3).
  * msal `oauth2cli/assertion.py` lines 125-130: `except:` (125), `logger.exception(` (127, logs at ERROR), `raise` (130) (L-2).
* Rationale:
  * M2: a custom `x-` header is not the standard `Auto-Submitted` header that mail systems check (RFC 3834, cited in D-037; not fetched again in this step). It would not reliably suppress automatic replies.
  * I3: the 16 KiB check limits what the application stores and processes, not what the server receives. The body limit must therefore be set in front of Django.
* Security implications:
  * M1: refusing a test that would not test delivery avoids a false result. The attempt is logged.
  * I2: the test email goes only to the requesting administrator's own address. The risk is mail and log volume for that address, for example by a compromised administrator account.
  * I3: without a body limit in front of Django, large uploads can use disk space and processing time before the 16 KiB check refuses them (denial of service).
  * L-2 (accepted residual risk, 2026-10-14): on a signing failure, msal logs with `logger.exception` at ERROR (`msal/oauth2cli/assertion.py` lines 125-130); the log entry can contain the exception text. Likelihood is low because the certificate and key pass checks at upload. The exception texts of PyJWT and cryptography were not verified (unverified). The owner may set the logger `msal.oauth2cli.assertion` to CRITICAL in `leg_platform/settings.py` (same as the key `msal.oauth2cli.oauth2` at line 371); this is not implemented.
* Alternatives:
  * M2: a separate SMTP path for the header (not chosen: D-037 covers the SMTP path only, and the Graph path is the chosen sender, D-006).
  * I2: a rate limit in the view (not built). The module `leg_platform/accounts/rate_limits.py` exists; whether it fits the test send is not checked.
  * requests: a lower minimum version without a verified support source (rejected: AGENTS.md, section 3, requires a verified source before a minimum is declared; the declared minimum is 2.34.2, see Decision).
* Sources (official sources or verification steps): Microsoft Learn, message resource type (see above); Django settings reference (see above); the `requests` pages and the installed package metadata (see above); D-006, D-018, D-037, D-044; AGENTS.md, section 3.
* Consequences:
  * Code, `pyproject.toml` and `requirements/minimum-constraints.txt` changed as listed under Decision (`requests` 2.34.2 declared in both).
  * `docs/INSTALLATION.md` now contains the verified sentence on the `x-` prefix (Microsoft Graph sender, before the Auto-Submitted note) and the upload size paragraph (Microsoft Graph sender).
  * The wording "only when their names start with `x-`" in D-006 is a reading of the Microsoft sentence. It is not changed by this entry.
  * Tests added for F1 to F5 and the console-mode gap (not run; `manage.py test` was excluded in this step): `TokenSessionTests.test_explicit_timeout_none_is_replaced_by_the_graph_timeout` (F4); `GraphConfigurationTests.test_non_ascii_private_key_is_refused_and_not_stored` (F2a); `GraphEmailBackendTests.test_non_ascii_private_key_is_refused_without_token_request` (F2b); `GraphEmailBackendTests.test_discovery_failure_in_constructor_is_raised_without_tenant_or_url` (F3); `GraphAdminTests.test_post_to_send_test_in_console_mode_is_refused_without_contacting_microsoft` (console-mode gap); `test_msal_response_bodies_are_not_logged_below_critical` in `leg_platform/tests/test_instance_configuration.py` (F5).
  * Open: the web server body limit value; the rate limit for the test send; whether Graph accepts `Auto-Submitted` at runtime (not tested); a lower minimum of `requests` (needs a verified support source); the F2a PEM-loader question.

## D-049: Implementation Sequence for Address Validation (D-033)

* Date: 2026-10-14
* Status: Decided under the project owner's directive of 2026-10-14; step A done (licence confirmed by the owner, see open points); step B not started.
* Context: D-033 is part of phase 1 (docs/PROJECT.md, "User Functions"). It requires a second read-only database alias, a separate import account, a router, migrations for that alias, and a verified data source. The owner's terms statement and the CSV columns are recorded below. The licence label of the dataset is still in contradiction (see open points). The update interval is not verified; the update trigger is an owner decision (D-050).
* Decision:
  1. Step A (research, no application code): verify the terms of use text and attribution obligation from https://www.swisstopo.admin.ch/ogd-conditions; verify the CSV asset columns from the STAC item https://data.geo.admin.ch/api/stac/v0.9/collections/ch.swisstopo.amtliches-gebaeudeadressverzeichnis/items (read the asset metadata; download the file only into $env:TEMP if the columns cannot be read otherwise; never store it in the repository). Record the results in D-049 with the URL and the date. If a fact cannot be verified, state "unverified".
  2. Step B (implementation): starts only after step A is recorded as verified. Second database alias, router with read-only access for the application and a separate import command with its own account; tests use a small fixture CSV in the repository, never the real dataset; migrations for the alias; the import verifies the SHA-256 multihash from the catalogue before use.
  3. Step C (implementation): the warning in registration and profile editing and the autocompletion endpoint. The endpoint queries only the local copy, is rate-limited, and gives no information about accounts. It is reachable without login, so it must be covered by the rate-limit rules of docs/SECURITY.md, section 3.
  4. The stored profile address stays exactly as entered (D-033). No address is sent to any external service. Downloading the register file from data.geo.admin.ch is an external call; it happens only as set in D-050 (option C).
  5. Owner items that the AI does not decide: the import account credentials and their server setup, the import schedule per server, and who acts as the operator of stage and production.
  6. The application account does not receive the `REFERENCES` privilege (`docs/INSTALLATION.md`, "Database privileges"). Reason: the MariaDB documentation for GRANT marks `REFERENCES` as "Unused" in the column-privilege table (the only table row fetched). Verified on 2026-10-14 (tester, local MariaDB 12.3, application account): `SHOW GRANTS` lists no `REFERENCES`; `manage.py test --noinput` with this account ran 791 tests, result OK. Not verified: the same on MariaDB 10.11 (CI minimum job runs the same suite; check in the next CI run).
* Sources: docs/DECISIONS.md D-033; docs/PROJECT.md "User Functions" and "Supported Devices"; docs/SECURITY.md sections 3 and 7.
* Open points:
  * Step A results (2026-10-14):
  * Terms of use (verified via the project owner's statement, source permitted under AGENTS.md section 3.3; the page https://www.swisstopo.admin.ch/de/nutzungsbedingungen-kostenlose-geodaten-und-geodienste could not be fetched by the tools): free geodata and geodata services may be used, distributed, made accessible, enriched, edited and used commercially. Attribution is mandatory for digital and analogue presentations and publications and for any passing on; one of these texts must be shown: "Bundesamt für Landestopografie swisstopo", "Office fédéral de topographie swisstopo", "Ufficio federale di topografia swisstopo", "Uffizi federal da topografia swisstopo", "Federal Office of Topography swisstopo", "©swisstopo".
  * Licence (decided 2026-10-14 by the owner, who copied the terms text): the STAC collection https://data.geo.admin.ch/api/stac/v0.9/collections/ch.swisstopo.amtliches-gebaeudeadressverzeichnis states "license":"proprietary" and links to https://www.swisstopo.admin.ch/ogd-conditions as "Terms of use". Decision: free use is allowed and the source must be named, with the text "Bundesamt für Landestopografie swisstopo" (owner's copy of the swisstopo terms, 2026-10-14). This matches the general opendata.swiss terms symbol "Open use. Must provide the source." (https://opendata.swiss/en/terms-of-use, fetched 2026-10-14). The STAC label "proprietary" stays a metadata difference and is not used as the legal basis.
  * Dataset facts (verified 2026-10-14 by download into a temporary folder, not stored in the repository): the Swiss CSV asset amtliches-gebaeudeadressverzeichnis_ch_2056.csv.zip is 143,324,478 bytes; its SHA-256 equals the catalogue multihash 1220D85F85AC36115E492F77E9C7FB2D567E1AA0D5EAFF57E2EFEFFBA302DC6F164F (prefix 1220 = SHA-256 multihash); it contains one CSV file of 468,445,908 bytes, UTF-8 with BOM, delimiter ";", 3,304,242 data lines.
  * CSV header (verified): ADR_EGAID;STR_ESID;BDG_EGID;ADR_EDID;STN_LABEL;ADR_NUMBER;BDG_CATEGORY;BDG_NAME;ZIP_LABEL;COM_FOSNR;COM_NAME;COM_CANTON;ADR_STATUS;ADR_OFFICIAL;ADR_MODIFIED;ADR_EASTING;ADR_NORTHING. There are no separate postal code or locality columns: ZIP_LABEL holds "<postal code> <locality>" (example "4052 Basel"); COM_NAME holds the municipality.
  * Unverified: update interval (STAC shows updated 2026-10-14; the schedule is not stated), the meaning of ADR_STATUS values, and whether BDG_NAME or STN_LABEL can contain person names (must be checked before storage in the second database).
  * Consequence for step B: the import reads ZIP_LABEL and COM_NAME and must check the SHA-256 multihash before use, as decided. Storage of the full CSV is not decided; only the columns needed for validation and autocomplete are to be stored (data minimisation, FADP art. 6, to be documented in step B).
  * Verification attempts on 2026-10-14: the terms pages https://www.swisstopo.admin.ch/ogd-conditions and https://www.swisstopo.admin.ch/de/nutzungsbedingungen-kostenlose-geodaten-und-geodienste could not be read by the fetch tool or the browser (connection reset). The STAC metadata still states "license":"proprietary"; the geocat.ch record (uuid 150c38c5-2650-4018-b5c7-939e2cdd56ce) shows distributor swisstopo, update frequency "As needed" and no licence field. The owner confirmed free use with source attribution on 2026-10-14 (see the licence bullet below).
